- **⚠ A behaviour fix rode along:** the pan-at-fit reset sat *inside* the scroll branch, so a left-drag at fit really could push a fitting image off-centre into grey with nothing putting it back until the next scroll. Unconditional now.
- **`Settings.pan_drag`, three values** — middle-drag (default), right-drag, Space+left-drag. ⚠ **Scroll and two-finger swipe always pan and are deliberately NOT configurable**: they are the universal route, and making them optional would let someone lock themselves out of panning entirely. ⚠ Configurable *now* not for urgency — trackpad and Magic Mouse users are already served by scroll — but because **the pan branch is written exactly once**: against a small enum it costs ~5 lines more than a hardcoded button, where retrofitting means rewriting shipped behaviour plus the settings row anyway. ⚠ `SpaceLeft` is gated on `memory().focused()`, or a space typed into the preset-name field arms panning.
- **⚠ The toolbar hint names the *configured* binding** via `PanDrag::label()`, never the word "middle" literally — otherwise it becomes a lie the moment the setting changes. Same shape as `Mask::label()`.
- **Two pure functions in `view_region`** (`zoom_step`, `zoom_anchor_shift`) taking **plain tuples, not `egui::Vec2`** — that module's own doc says it is deliberately egui-free so it can be tested without a window. ⚠ `zooming_out_shifts_the_opposite_way` exists so that negating the whole anchor expression cannot pass.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) **a habit change felt on every photo** — the point, not a side effect; (2) **two-finger swipe at fit does nothing**, because there is nothing to reveal, and it is the caveat most likely to be reported as a bug; (3) no kinetic/inertial panning; (4) three bindings, not general rebinding; (5) the hint is Develop-only; (6) **ctrl+scroll rather than cmd+scroll** on macOS, inherited from egui.

**M3.1b is complete — a radial gradient mask, and the verdict is that 3.1a's architecture held.** 582 tests, goldens 33 → 35, and the 33 came out byte-identical. ⚠ **M3.1 still does not close until 3.1c's brush.**

- **⚠⚠ The aspect correction does NOT come back, and that is the load-bearing finding.** `LinearGradient::coverage` needs an `aspect` because a projection needs a metric, and anisotropic scaling destroys *perpendicularity*. It does **not** destroy *axis-alignment*: an axis-aligned ellipse maps to an axis-aligned ellipse, so `((u−cx)/rx)² + ((v−cy)/ry)² ≤ 1` is already correct on screen with no correction whatsoever. **`RadialGradient::coverage` therefore takes `aspect` and ignores it** — guarded by `radial_coverage_is_independent_of_aspect`, whose only job is to stop a future reader "fixing" the omission that three sibling functions genuinely need.
- **⚠ Rotation is precisely what would bring the trap back** — a rotated ellipse under anisotropic scaling is still an ellipse but with different axes *and a different angle*, so a stored angle would not be the angle you see. That is the main argument for axis-aligned only, ahead of the extra handle and the extra field. Revisit only with that cost understood.
- **⚠ `mask_tool::hit_test` still needs the aspect**, because grabbing a handle is a screen-space distance test. So the two functions differ deliberately and each says so in its doc. **Do not "unify" them.**
- **Inside is covered; `invert` flips it.** Diverges from Lightroom, whose radial filter affects the outside by default — rejected because a new mask that does nothing where you drew it surprises anyone who has not used Lightroom.
- **⚠ `invert` had never had any UI.** It has existed on `LinearGradient` since 3.1a, is honoured by `coverage`, survives drags and is tested — but no control existed, so it was reachable only by hand-editing a sidecar. Survivable for a gradient (drag the endpoints past each other); **not** survivable for a radial, where the subject vignette depends on it and no drag substitutes. A shared checkbox now serves both, driven by `Mask::invert_mut() -> &mut bool`.
- **Feather is a 0–100 slider with a smoothstep ramp**, reusing the vignette's convention so the app has one feather idiom. ⚠ The linear gradient has **no** feather field — its ramp width *is* the distance between its handles — so this is a real asymmetry, not an oversight. Smoothstep rather than linear because a linear ramp in radial distance leaves a visible crease ring.
- **⚠ `RadialGradient`'s `Default` is hand-written where `LinearGradient`'s is derived.** Container-level `#[serde(default)]` fills a missing field from the **struct's** `Default`, so a derived one would give every partial sidecar a zero-radius point-ellipse — and `feather: 50` cannot come from `f32::default()` under any arrangement. Radius `[0.25, 0.25]` renders as an ellipse *similar to the frame*, since both components are fractions of their own axis.
- **⚠ The zero-radius guard returns 0.0, where the linear's returns 1.0.** The linear's choice was between full coverage and NaN; here the honest answer exists — an ellipse with no area covers nothing — and it falls through the invert, so an inverted point-ellipse covers everything. That is the limit of the surrounding behaviour, not a special case.
- **One flat `Handle` enum, and `hit_test`/`apply_drag` now take `Mask` rather than a shape.** `Session.active_mask_handle` is a single field that must hold either, and nesting (`Handle { Linear(..), Radial(..) }`) would **not** make a mismatched pair unrepresentable — only add a level. So `_ => m` covers the pair that cannot occur, and degrading to "the drag does nothing" is the right failure for a UI. ⚠ **3.1c will test this decision much harder**; see the NEXT ACTION note.
- **Five handles; an edge drag resizes about a fixed centre**, and the centre is hit-tested **before** the edges so a small ellipse can still be moved rather than only resized. Radii clamp to `[0.01, 2.0]` and the centre to `[0, 1]` — a shape dragged out of reach cannot be got back.
- **⚠ The plan's sequencing dissolved two of its own predictions, and the lesson generalises.** `mask_tool` was retargeted at `Mask` and `paint_mask` was split out of `draw_mask_overlay` **before** the variant existed, as pure refactors provable by unchanged tests. So the "three wanted compile errors" arrived as **one** (`paint_mask`'s match), and the irrefutable `let Mask::Linear(g) = m.mask` binding had already been deleted rather than becoming an error. **Preparing a seam converts a compile error into a match arm, which is a better place to make the decision.** The predicted `E0499` borrow trap also never fired: it needs two closures over the same local, which was 3.1a's shape, not this one.
- **⚠ `paint_mask` was split on *painting*, not on the whole function** — the drag plumbing (hit-test, apply, history push, `s.request`) stays written once. That is what makes the radial inherit **both** of 3.1a's real-use bug fixes structurally: the `s.request` call after a drag, and placement against `frame_rect` rather than `img_rect`. Duplicating the plumbing per shape is exactly how those two would come back.
- **The ellipse is a 64-point closed polyline** (`egui::Shape::closed_line`); egui has no ellipse primitive. ⚠ `ELLIPSE_SEGMENTS` had to land in the *same commit as its caller* — a `const` with no caller in a **binary** crate is dead code that fails `clippy -D warnings`, the third instance of that trap after `theme::button_row` and the CLI's `format_arg`.
- **Zero change** in `pipeline`, `MaskStage`, `EditGroups`, `merge_from`, the sidecar format, `edit_clipboard`, sync, presets or batch export. That is the evidence the architecture held, and the byte-identical 33 goldens are the proof.
- **⚠ Three tests could not detect what they named, plus a clipping golden** — all four in the standing-lesson list above. Read them; M3.1b produced half of that list's instances in one feature.
- **Validated on a real X-T4 `.RAF`, both directions, measured rather than squinted at.** Inverted at −1.5 EV: centre ratio **1.000**, corners and edges **0.52–0.60**. ⚠ **0.58 is *full* application, not partial** — −1.5 EV is ×0.354 in linear light, which sRGB-encodes to ≈0.61, so reading the linear figure off an encoded file would look like the mask was half working. Non-inverted at +1.2 EV and +60 saturation: the ellipse reads **1.483×** and every probe outside it is **1.000**, unchanged to the byte.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) **no rotation** — the trade that keeps `coverage` correction-free; (2) **no inner feather guide on the canvas**, so where the ramp *starts* is numeric only (a dashed inner ellipse is a contained follow-up); (3) one clone and full stage runs per mask even where coverage is zero, inherited from 3.1a — ⚠ radial is where a bounding-box optimisation would start to pay, and it is now deferred twice; (4) per-pixel adjustments only; (5) no mask intersection or subtraction, so a radial cannot be cut out of a linear; (6) **M3.1 does not close here**.

**M3.1a is complete — masks can now be authored on the canvas.** Plan B added `mask_tool`, draggable handles with guide lines, and a Masks panel section. 559 tests at the time. ⚠ **M3.1 still does not close until 3.1c's brush.**

- **No new mode, and the precedence rule is finer than the spec first said.** Handles appear when a mask is selected in the list; `geometry_mode` still wins while on. ⚠ But the spec's "otherwise the mask owns the drag" would have **killed panning**, because the non-geometry branch owns pan and zoom. The real rule: a drag belongs to the mask **only if it started on a handle**, which is what `active_mask_handle` records. Found by reading the canvas while writing the plan, not by shipping it broken.
- **⚠⚠ The plan's own sabotage step was a lie, and a subagent caught it.** `hit_testing_the_line_is_aspect_correct` used a **horizontal** axis with the probe point directly below it — where the perpendicular distance is purely vertical, so scaling x cannot change the answer. It passed with the aspect correction deleted: a test that named the bug it could not detect. Now a **vertical** axis with horizontally-offset probes, which genuinely discriminates (`Some(Line)` vs `None`). ⚠ Same lesson as the rotate/flip work: *a property test is only as good as the property*. Both times the test looked rigorous and checked nothing.
- **⚠ The aspect correction now appears in three places** — `LinearGradient::coverage`, `mask_tool::hit_test`, and the canvas's perpendicular guide lines. Each measures distance in **screen** space; normalised space is anisotropic and skews all three.
- **⚠ `mask_tool`'s `tol` is in frame heights; `crop_tool`'s is normalised.** Two siblings, same signature shape, different units — a tolerance copied across is wrong by the aspect ratio. Documented on `hit_test` after a review caught the silence.
- **Guide lines, not a coverage tint.** A faithful tint needs *three* quads because coverage is clamped flat either side of the ramp; two boundary lines say the same thing exactly and do not wash out the photograph. A tint behind a toggle stays available.
- **`selected_mask` is view state on `Session`, never in `Edits`** — a selection is not part of the photograph's description, so it must not reach the sidecar or transfer on a paste. Per-photo, so it resets on a switch for free.
- **⚠ The borrow trap fired exactly where the plan predicted.** `E0499`: the Masks section both wrote `settled = true` and called the `slider` closure, which independently captures `&mut settled`. Fixed by the codebase's established pattern — a local flag folded in after the closure closes — **not** `RefCell`.
- **⚠ `selection_after_delete` clears rather than moves.** Deleting the selected mask selects nothing, following `Selection::clamp`'s rule for the same reason: silently selecting a *different* mask means the next slider drag edits something the user never chose. A review also caught that deleting must clear `active_mask_handle`, or the canvas's early return leaves a stale handle and panning dead.
- **⚠ `let Mask::Linear(g) = m.mask` is irrefutable only while `Mask` has one variant.** 3.1b makes it a compile error — which is right: the overlay must then decide what to draw.
- **⚠⚠ Two bugs found in the first minutes of real use, and both are worth reading before touching the canvas.**
  - **The image did not update while dragging a handle** — it kept showing the mask's previous position until some *other* edit happened to request a frame, which is why moving a slider appeared to "fix" it. `draw_mask_overlay` mutated `Edits` but never called `s.request`. ⚠ The cause is an inherited false analogy: it was modelled on `draw_crop_overlay`, which needs no request *because* `preview_edits` strips the crop in geometry mode, so the overlay **is** the feedback. Masks are not stripped. Copying a structure without checking why its parts exist.
  - **The mask drifted with the view when zoomed**, following the visible part of the image while panning. The overlay mapped normalised coordinates onto `img_rect` — but once a region render is on, `img_rect` covers only the **rendered slice**, not the frame. Now placed against a `frame_rect` derived from the same whole-image origin the region placement uses, so the two agree by construction.
  - ⚠ **That second bug had three heads and only one was visible.** `aspect` was computed from `img_rect` too — a *region's* aspect, not the frame's — which silently corrupted hit-testing at high zoom; and the drag deltas were divided by the region's dimensions, so a handle dragged while zoomed moved by the wrong amount. Both would have surfaced later as "handles feel wrong when zoomed" with no obvious cause. **Every coordinate in that function is frame-relative now; `img_rect` survives only as the clip.**
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) linear gradients only; (2) no coverage tint; (3) **the panel's placement is provisional** — this is the concept that parked the layout work; (4) no mask reordering or renaming; (5) no keyboard nudging.

**Phase 3 started. M3.1a's headless half is done: a linear-gradient mask carrying per-pixel adjustments renders correctly, with no GUI involved.**

- **Phase 3 is four independent subsystems and was decomposed before anything was specced.** M3.1 masking first — everything recent was preparation for it, and it unblocks the parked layout work. ⚠ **M3.2** lens corrections needs lensfun, a new C dependency with the same build risk libraw was. ⚠ **M3.3** colour grading has a *hidden prerequisite*: real colour management does not exist — lcms2 emits one static sRGB profile and nothing else. **M3.4** virtual copies is the smallest and most independent.
- **⚠ M3.1 is itself sliced into three**: **3.1a** the architecture on one analytic mask (this), **3.1b** radial, **3.1c** brush. M3.1 **does not close until 3.1c** — its own "Done when" names dodge/burn with a brush.
- **⚠ And 3.1a is two plans.** This is plan A, headless. `pipeline` has zero GUI dependency by rule and the CLI is the permanent test harness, so the whole architecture — coordinate space, region-awareness, composition, blending, export — was proved against goldens and a real `.RAF` **before any canvas code exists**. Plan B is the UI. That also means a canvas bug can never be confused with a model bug.
- **A mask carries the per-pixel adjustments only** — temp/tint, exposure, contrast, highlights/shadows/whites/blacks, saturation/vibrance. ⚠ Not an arbitrary subset: it is exactly the set where "apply the stage, then blend by coverage" is correct *and* cheap, because each output pixel depends only on its own input. A spatial stage reads neighbours **outside** the mask, so masked NR/sharpening/clarity/dehaze/HSL each need their own decision and are out of scope.
- **`MaskStage` runs ordinary stages on a clone and blends.** Every existing stage is reused **unchanged** — nothing else learns masking exists, keeping the `PipelineStage` seam opaque for the GPU port. Fusing into one per-pixel closure was rejected for exactly that reason. A bounding-box optimisation is deferred to 3.1b/c, where masks are small enough to pay.
- **`per_pixel_stages` is one list with two consumers** — the global tail and every mask. ⚠ The global tail passes **zero** saturation/vibrance, because in the global chain saturation runs *after* the tone curve and local contrast; passing the real values would silently move it earlier and change every golden.
- **⚠ Coverage takes the frame aspect, and that is not decoration.** A *line* through two points survives anisotropic scaling but lines *perpendicular* to it do not, so raw normalised coordinates skew a diagonal gradient's iso-lines on screen. Watched failing: dropping the correction gives 0.4665 and 0.5335 for two points that must be equal. Same species as the vignette's `roundness`.
- **⚠⚠ Masking exposed a latent bug in `tail_kernel_radius`: the reaches *add*, they do not `max`.** Sharpening runs on local contrast's **output**, so a wrong value at a region edge travels 120 px through clarity and a further 9–15 px through sharpening. It was `sharp.max(local)`, and `a_region_render_matches_the_whole_render_cropped` had been passing with an inset ~9 px too small — the residual differences happened to round the same way. A masked +0.6 EV (up to 1.5× gain) pushed them across a quantisation boundary and it failed. ⚠ **The old test asserted the wrong value with the wrong reasoning written into its comment** ("each reads its own neighbourhood of that stage's input"). Both are corrected. **Production was never at risk**: `REGION_MARGIN` is 192 against a true worst case of 135 — the slack was in the margin, the deficit was in the test.
- **⚠ Masks are the seventh `EditGroups` group and default to OFF**, like geometry: a gradient positioned for one composition is meaningless on another. `merge_from`'s exhaustive destructure made that a compile error, as did both exhaustive fixtures (`pipeline::all_edits`, `edit_clipboard`'s payload).
- **Validated end to end on the X-T4 `.RAF`** by hand-writing a sidecar and rendering it: with `invert: false` the top measures 84.1 and the bottom 19.5; inverted, 52.9 and 33.2. Each image is darker exactly where its mask has coverage. ⚠ The two look nearly identical by eye at reduced size — measured, not squinted at.
- **Goldens 31 → 33**, and the 31 stayed byte-identical, which is the proof `MaskStage` is inert with no masks. ⚠ The exposure golden's right quarter **clips** (the chart already ends at 253), so it cannot discriminate coverage 0.75 from 1.0; the midrange does — 138→153 at ¼, 189→226 at ½.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) one full-image clone and full stage runs per mask, even where coverage is zero; (2) per-pixel adjustments only; (3) no mask intersection, subtraction, or range/colour masks; (4) **M3.1 does not close here**; (5) masks do not transfer by default.

**Rustroom has a designed look now: dark by default, a switchable accent, compact controls.** `theme.rs` stopped being an empty seam. Settled over three side-by-side comparisons in the brainstorming visual companion rather than from description.

- **⚠ The argument that narrowed the palette is not taste.** A photo editor's UI is the *surround* you judge the image against, and surround affects perceived tone and saturation — which is why Lightroom, Capture One and darktable are all dark and near-neutral. It argues for low chroma **near the image** and says nothing about panels further away, which is where an accent can live safely.
- **⚠ Amber is deliberately absent from the accent list**, on a functional objection rather than an aesthetic one: it sits on the skin-tone and colour-temperature axis, which is precisely the judgement Temp and Tint exist to serve, so a warm cast becomes harder to see against it. It also collides with amber's conventional meaning as a warning. One line to add if that is ever overruled.
- **⚠ Each accent is a *pair*, not a value.** A colour tuned for near-black washes out on near-white — `#7fb0d8` on a light base measures **2.17:1**, against a 3:1 threshold. Eight constants, not four. `every_accent_has_usable_contrast` sweeps `Accent::ALL` × both themes; **watched failing** by swapping the light steel blue for the dark one, which took `every_accent_differs_between_themes` down with it.
- **The four pairs were contrast-computed *before* being written down** — worst case 5.48:1 against egui's real bases (`panel_fill` is `gray(27)` / `gray(248)`). So the test is a guard for the *next* accent someone adds, not a hurdle this change had to scramble over.
- **⚠ The choice lives in the lib; the colour lives in the bin.** `Settings` is in `rustroom_app`, which must not depend on egui, so `ThemeChoice` and `Accent` are plain serde enums there and `theme.rs` maps them. ⚠ `ThemeChoice` therefore **mirrors `egui::ThemePreference`**, which this file elsewhere warns against — accepted because the direction is forced, with three exhaustive match arms as the mitigation.
- **⚠ `accent_color` is a free function, not `impl Accent`.** Rust's orphan rule allows inherent impls only in the crate defining the type, and `Accent` is in the lib — so it was an extension trait or a free function, and a trait carrying one method used in one file is machinery for its own sake.
- **⚠⚠ egui's defaults are already tighter than they look, and the first "compact" values were *looser*.** `item_spacing.y` defaults to **3.0** and `interact_size.y` to **18.0**; the plan set 4.0 and 18.0 — one larger, one identical. `the_compact_metrics_are_tighter_than_the_defaults` caught it. Now 2.0 / 16.0, and the visible gain comes mostly from **halving `slider_rail_height` 8.0 → 4.0**, which is where the vertical space in a panel of sliders actually goes. Lesson: assert the *direction* of a metric change, because "compact" is meaningless without knowing the baseline.
- **⚠ `base_fill` lives in the test module**, not production — it is only asked by the contrast test, and a `pub fn` with no production caller is a `dead_code` error in a **binary** crate. Third time that trap has bitten this session.
- **Changing either setting re-applies immediately**, guarded by a change comparison because the Settings dialog body runs every frame — the same rule `apply_view_demosaic` follows, now stated twice in `modals/mod.rs`'s doc.
- **⚠ Accepted caveats:** (1) **two palettes to maintain** — every future colour decision is made twice; (2) `Neutral` is genuinely harder to read, since active state falls back to lightness alone; (3) compact costs some trackpad precision on fine drags; (4) **the accent reaches only egui's own widget colours** — the crop overlay, the histogram and the thumbnail selection border are painted by hand with hardcoded colours and do not follow it, which matters most in **light** mode where they have never been seen; (5) **no icons** — everything stays text, recorded as a decision taken rather than an omission, since that is the *structural* half that gets expensive to defer.

**The canvas split done — `main.rs` is a state machine now, not a view.** **2,762 → 1,345**; no file in the crate over 341 lines. Together with the modal split, `main.rs` went **3,419 → 1,345** in one session.

- **`develop_body` (380 lines) became three files**, and this was a decomposition rather than a move: `develop/panel.rs` (the adjustments), `develop/canvas.rs` (image, zoom/pan, crop overlay), `develop/mod.rs` (orchestration + toolbar + preset panel). Moving it intact was rejected — masking adds a mask list to the panel *and* overlays to the canvas, so an intact move would just hand the next person a 380-line function with masks bolted on.
- **⚠ `photo_edited` became a return value, and that is what made the split clean.** It was a local threaded through all 380 lines because both write sites sit inside closures already holding `&mut self` through `self.session`. It is written in **exactly one place per half** — a slider settling in the panel, `draw_crop_overlay` in the canvas — so `panel::show(app, ui) -> bool` and `canvas::show(app, ui, ctx) -> bool` each report for themselves and `body` ORs them into one action-log entry. One gesture stays one Ctrl+Z. This is the shape `draw_crop_overlay` already used.
- **⚠ Panel order is load-bearing** and is now documented on `develop::body`: egui allocates side panels in call order and the central panel takes what is left, so preset (left) → adjustments (right) → filmstrip (bottom) → canvas (central) must be preserved exactly.
- **`thumb.rs` is the unit the *tests* found.** `thumb::cell` is shared by the Library grid and the filmstrip, and six of `main.rs`'s tests clustered around it. ⚠ Those tests **reimplement the layout loop rather than driving the real one**, so a pure move cannot break them — their passing is *not* evidence about this refactor. They moved because they document `cell`'s rules and belong beside it.
- **⚠ `shell.rs` is the one move where `self` stays `self`.** An inherent/trait impl only has to be in the same *crate*, not the same module, so `impl eframe::App for RustroomApp` relocated verbatim — no `self.` → `app.` substitution, unlike every other move here.
- **`theme::caption` landed with both call sites** — the camera model in the develop panel, the filename under a Library thumbnail. ⚠ Deliberately **not** merged with `hint` despite rendering identically: a hint is explanatory text *under a control*, a caption labels a thing, and merging two idioms because they currently look alike is how a design system loses the distinctions it exists to hold.
- **Zero visible change**, unlike the modal split's four hint labels. Anything a user can see is a regression.
- **⚠ Accepted caveat: `main.rs` at 1,345 is still the largest file**, holding the state types, decode functions and lifecycle orchestration — decode pumping, export, undo/redo, sync. That is a plausible **third** split and is deliberately *not* proposed: unlike the canvas, it is not where M3.1 lands. Do it when it hurts.

**The modal dialogs live in `modals/` now, and `theme` is the seam the app's look will come through.** `main.rs` **3,419 → 2,762**; nine dialogs into five files; all 527 tests green (524 + theme's 3).

- **⚠ Both `theme` and `modals` are *binary* modules, declared in `main.rs`** — not lib modules. `lib.rs` says "no egui here — unit-testable units live in this lib", and that separation is what keeps `history`, `selection`, `action_log` testable without a windowing system. Consequence: `pub` does not escape the binary, so **every item must have a real caller or `clippy -D warnings` fails on dead code** — the same trap the CLI's `format_arg` hit. That is why `button_row` was introduced in the same step as its first caller rather than up front.
- **⚠⚠ `Context::set_style` does not exist in egui 0.35 — styles are *per theme*.** The plan was written against an API that isn't there. Rustroom never calls `set_theme`, so it follows the system and **both dark and light are live**. `theme::apply` therefore loops over both with `style_mut_of`, mutating in place: handing over a whole `Style` with `set_style_of` would overwrite that theme's own `visuals` and give light mode dark colours. `both_themes_are_tuned` exists because a tweak applied to one theme only is invisible until someone switches their system appearance. **This matters for the visual identity work: it is two palettes, not one.**
- **Free functions taking `&mut RustroomApp`, not an `impl` block in another file.** The latter would have been a cut-and-paste with zero call-site churn — which is exactly what makes it a file move dressed as a refactor. Per-dialog input/output structs were also rejected: the testable logic (`Settings::apply_delete_confirm`, `batch_export::destination_ready`) was *already* extracted, so what remains is glue, and wrapping glue in ceremony makes it longer without making it safer.
- **⚠ Privacy runs in two directions and both came up.** `modals` reaches `RustroomApp`'s **private fields** with no `pub` anywhere, because privacy is "visible in the declaring module *and its descendants*". But `main.rs` is the **ancestor** of `modals::settings`, so the submodules themselves needed `pub mod`. The spec's "no visibility changes needed" was true of the fields and false of the modules.
- **⚠ The audit that changed the spec: the dialog conventions were *already* uniform.** An earlier draft claimed inconsistent Close-vs-Cancel and inconsistent button order and proposed fixing both. Checking all nine first showed confirm-first/dismiss-second everywhere, and "Close" only in Settings — the one dialog with nothing to abandon. So `theme::button_row` **encodes** the existing convention so it cannot drift, rather than imposing a new one, and Settings keeps its plain `ui.button("Close")` rather than being forced through a helper with an always-disabled empty confirm.
- **⚠ `.small()` serves two roles, and flattening them would have been a mistake.** Eight sites: **four are hints** (explanatory text under a control, all in dialogs) and became `theme::hint`; **two are captions** (a filename under a thumbnail, a camera model beside a title) and were deliberately left — they live in `develop_body`/`library_view`, which belong to the canvas split, so `theme::caption` arrives there with its call sites; two are test fixtures.
- **The borrow idiom is now written down** in `modals/mod.rs` rather than rediscovered: copy state into locals, run the closure, then apply — the closure cannot touch `app` because `dialog` already holds it. Plus the rule that **a dialog body runs every frame it is open**, so any side effect needs a change guard.
- **Only one visible change shipped**: hint-label spacing at four sites. Anything else a user can see is a regression.
- **⚠ Accepted caveat: `main.rs` is still 2,762 lines.** The modals were only 19% of it. `develop_body` alone is 380, and the canvas is where masking lands — which is why the canvas split, not this one, is the next action.

**The 1:1 view's demosaic tier is now a setting — and mirroring the export preset was considered and rejected.** Closes caveat (6) of the full-resolution work, but not the way that caveat assumed.

- **⚠ The rejected design is the useful part of this entry.** "Mirror the export preset" fails on three counts. (1) **There is no mismatch by default** — `linear_scene()` hardcodes `Fast` and `ExportOptions::default()` is also `Fast`, so the gap only opens if you deliberately pick a slower tier. (2) **"The export preset's demosaic" is not one value** — there are N named presets plus `settings.export` as the last-used, and the same photo legitimately goes out as web-JPEG-fast *and* archive-TIFF-best; any automatic choice makes the view's decode cost depend on distant state. (3) **The cost is recurring, not per-export** — the full-res decode is eager, so mirroring `Best` would take every photo you pause on from 2.89 s to 11.42 s, for a difference validated on 2026-08-11 as "marginally crisper hair-strand separation".
- **⚠ And a proportion argument worth keeping:** for the first ~4 s after opening a photo the buffer is the half-size decode, which skips demosaic **entirely** — 2×2 binning, not a worse algorithm but none at all. That is caveat (3) of the same feature and nobody minds. Fretting over Fast-vs-Best while accepting that is straining at a gnat.
- **So: `Settings.view_demosaic`, an explicit choice, defaulting to `Fast`.** Nobody who ignores it sees any change.
- **⚠ The tier is *captured in the closure*, not passed per request.** `DecodeWorker`'s `decode` is an injected `Arc<dyn Fn(&Path)>` shared with the **preview** decode — where demosaic is inert, because libraw skips it for a half-size decode. Putting a tier on `DecodeWorker::request` would impose a parameter on a caller that cannot act on it. So `full_res_worker(tier, ctx)` builds the worker, and changing the setting rebuilds it; dropping the old one disconnects its channel and an in-flight decode discards its own result — the abandon-don't-abort trade already used at handover. A shared `Arc<AtomicU8>` was the other option and was rejected as mutable state at a distance for something that changes a handful of times a session.
- **Changing it re-decodes the open photo**, so the setting visibly does something rather than deferring to the next photo. `apply_view_demosaic` persists, rebuilds, clears `full_pending` and sets `buffer_is_full_res = false` for a **RAW** session; `maybe_request_full_res` then fires next frame because `opened_at` is long past `SETTLE`. The old buffer stays on screen throughout.
- **⚠ `full_pending` must be cleared, and the reason is not obvious**: that sequence number belongs to the worker being dropped, so leaving it makes `pump_full_res` wait on a number no live worker will produce — which blocks `maybe_request_full_res`, since it refuses while a decode is in flight.
- **⚠ The one-frame trap:** `settings_modal` runs *every frame* the dialog is open, so `if tier != self.settings.view_demosaic` is what stops a worker rebuild and an 11 s decode firing sixty times a second.
- **⚠ The hint is a multiplier, not seconds.** The export dialog's "roughly 1.2 s to 11 s per photo" are X-T4 numbers written into prose and wrong on any other body. `view_quality_hint` says "about 4× the decode time of Fast" instead — ratios are the stable part. It lives in `app`, not on `raw_decode::Demosaic`: a cost measured on one camera is presentation, not a property of the algorithm.
- **The export dialog's hint was half false and is fixed.** "Not shown in the preview, which skips demosaic entirely" holds for the fit preview before the full-res buffer lands, and not for the 1:1 view once it has.
- **Testing is thin and deliberately not padded** — most of this is egui glue and worker rebuilding. Two tests earn their place: `a_settings_file_from_before_view_demosaic_still_loads` (⚠ `SettingsStore::load()` is infallible, so a field that fails to parse silently reverts **every** setting — watched failing by removing `#[serde(default)]`, which took five tests down) and `every_demosaic_tier_has_a_cost_hint`, a table over `Demosaic::ALL` so a fifth tier cannot ship showing nothing.
- **⚠ Two pre-existing call sites the plan missed, both caught by the compiler**: the exhaustive `Settings { .. }` literal in `settings_round_trip_carries_the_export_options` (kept exhaustive on purpose — that is what makes the *next* field a compile error there), and the gated `a_full_size_decode_is_twice_the_half_size_one`, which calls `decode_full_res` directly.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) a non-default tier is paid on **every photo you dwell on**, not per export; (2) changing the setting spends a full decode immediately; (3) the handover drops the prefix cache, so the first render after it is cold — identical to the original upgrade; (4) it does nothing for JPEG/PNG/TIFF, which have no demosaic step; (5) **it still does not guarantee the view matches a given export, and is not meant to** — it lets you choose what to inspect at; (6) it does not apply to the first ~4 s after opening, when the buffer is the binned half-size decode.

**`rustroom render --format` done — the CLI writes all five formats now.** Closes caveat (4) of the PNG/TIFF work: `export::save` had dispatched five formats since then, and the CLI passed `ExportFormat::Jpeg` literally.

- **The output *path* decides, and `--format` refines.** `.jpg`/`.jpeg` → JPEG, `.png` → PNG 8-bit, `.tif`/`.tiff` → TIFF 8-bit; the flag supplies the depth an extension cannot express. Flag-only was rejected because `render in.RAF out.png` would then silently write a JPEG named `.png`; extension-only was rejected because it makes `Png16` and `Tiff16` unreachable — an ambiguity that is *why* `ExportFormat` is flat rather than `format` + `bit_depth`.
- **⚠ A flag contradicting a *recognised* extension is an error, not a silently mislabelled file.** But a flag against an extension we cannot read (`out.foo --format png8`) is honoured: the user was explicit, and second-guessing that is worse than the odd filename.
- **⚠ The compatibility check compares `ExportFormat::extension()` on both sides, never the raw strings.** `Jpeg.extension()` is `"jpg"` while users write `out.jpeg` — so inference normalises first, and one function does both jobs. That is also what lets `--format png16` into `out.png` pass (both `"png"`, only the depth differs) while `tiff16` into `out.png` fails.
- **⚠ Deliberate asymmetry: inference accepts aliases, the flag does not.** `.jpeg` and `.tiff` are things a user already has; the flag takes exactly five tokens and the error lists them, exactly as `--demosaic` does. `token_list()` builds that list from `TOKENS` so a sixth format cannot be missing from one of the two error messages.
- **`app::format_arg` lives in the CLI, not in `export`** — a command-line token vocabulary is a CLI concern, the same trade already written into `--demosaic`'s comment. `export` never learns about `clap`.
- **⚠ `--quality` became `Option<u8>`** so "explicitly passed" stays distinguishable from "defaulted" — which is the only way `--quality 50 --format tiff8` can warn that it is inert rather than silently ignoring it. The default (92) moved into `DEFAULT_JPEG_QUALITY`.
- **The format resolves *before* the decode**, so a typo fails in milliseconds instead of after a multi-second decode and render. Verified: a bad `--format` with a nonexistent input fails on the format, and a good one fails on the decode.
- **⚠ It is one commit, not two, and the reason generalises.** The plan first split it into "add the tested module" then "wire it in" — but a `pub fn` in a **binary** crate reaches nothing outside the crate, so a `format_arg` nobody calls is dead code in the literal compiler sense and `clippy --all-targets -D warnings` fails on the bin target (the `#[cfg(test)]` uses exist only in the test target). Splitting would have needed an `#[allow(dead_code)]` put in and taken straight back out.
- **13 tests — the first this crate has ever had.** A bin-only crate takes `#[cfg(test)]` modules like any other. `a_flag_contradicting_a_known_extension_is_refused` was **watched failing** with the guard stubbed to `if false`.
- **Validated on a real X-T4 `.RAF`**: `--format png16` produced `bitsPerSample: 16` per `sips` — checking the *depth* is the point, since a silent 8-bit downgrade opens fine and looks right, the exact failure `sixteen_bit_output_really_carries_more_than_eight` exists for in `export`. The `out.jpg` regression case still writes an 8-bit JPEG.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) **`render in.RAF out.foo` now errors** where it used to write a JPEG — the deliberate behaviour change, and the common `out.jpg` case is untouched; (2) a flag with an unrecognised extension still produces a mislabelled file, by choice; (3) **`decode` is unchanged** — it writes a display-referred 8-bit TIFF by hand through `image::save_buffer` rather than through `export` at all, which is a debug dump rather than an export and a separate question.

**A photo's undo stack now survives a switch away and back, and Ctrl+Z is scoped to the photo on screen.** `History<Edits>` still lives on `Session`; what changed is that it no longer dies with it, and that `ActionLog` entries can now say *which* photo they belong to.

- **⚠ The storage was the easy half; the log was the feature.** A per-path history alone does nothing, because `ActionKind::PhotoEdit` was **anonymous** — it meant "pop whatever history is current", which after a switch is a different photo's, which is exactly why `forget_photo_edits()` had to delete every one of them on every switch. Restore A's stack without touching the log and Ctrl+Z is still dead. The variant carries a `PathBuf` now.
- **Undo is scoped to the open photo, not chronological across photos.** Lightroom's own answer is the other one — strict order, jumping back to the photo an undo belongs to — and it was rejected because a keystroke whose whole promise is "put it back" should not yank the view elsewhere and start a decode. Reverting a closed photo's sidecar in the background was rejected outright: it changes a photo the user cannot see. Global actions (`PresetDelete`, `Sync`) still interleave chronologically with whichever photo is open.
- **⚠ `ActionLog` is now one `Vec<Entry>` with an `undone` flag, not two stacks.** Filtering two stacks by reachability means removing an entry from the *middle* of one and pushing it back on the *top* of the other, which scrambles chronological order across photos — observable as a wrong undo order the next time you switch, and repairable only by putting a sequence number on every entry. The flag makes the ordering intrinsic. `undo` takes the **last** reachable entry in force; `redo` takes the **first** reachable undone one, and *first* is what makes them exact inverses: within one photo's subsequence the in-force entries form a prefix and the undone ones a suffix, so the first undone entry **is** the one undone most recently. With a single photo open throughout, this is behaviourally identical to the old two stacks, which is the regression argument — all 13 pre-existing tests passed with their assertions unchanged.
- **⚠ `reachable()` is where the semantics live.** Global actions are reachable from anywhere; a `PhotoEdit` only from its own photo; a **`Batch` only when every member is**, so one Ctrl+V stays one Ctrl+Z.
- **⚠ That fixes a wart rather than extending one.** The old sweep tore the `PhotoEdit` member out of every batch on every switch, **permanently** stranding the sidecar half of a paste with no way to undo it. Now the batch simply waits, intact, and becomes undoable again on return. `a_batch_survives_a_visit_to_another_photo_intact` pins it.
- **⚠ One rule invalidates a stale stack, checked at one moment.** A sync or paste rewrites the sidecars of photos that are *not open*, so their stashed histories describe a state no longer on disk. Rather than oblige every writer — `sync::apply`, `apply_to_selection`, a preset click, a `Sync` undo *and* redo, and anything added later — to remember to invalidate, `HistoryStash::take_matching(path, on_disk)` hands a history back **only if `stashed.get() == on_disk`** and drops it otherwise. That single check subsumes all of them. `Edits` is `PartialEq` over `f32`, so a `NaN` compares unequal and it fails toward "start fresh" — the safe direction, as `PrefixKey` fails toward a cache miss.
- **`app::edit_history::HistoryStash` is a stash, not a store.** `Session` keeps owning the authoritative history, so all ~10 `s.history.*` call sites were untouched. Making the stash authoritative would force every `s.history.push` — each already inside a closure holding `&mut self` through `self.session` — to take a second simultaneous `&mut self` borrow, which is the egui borrow shape this codebase has already paid compile errors for twice. Putting histories in `BufferCache` was rejected too: capped at 3 for memory, `Arc`-immutable, and a history should outlive its buffer.
- **`finalize_session` is the only place `self.session` is ever assigned**, so it is the only choke point needed — it stashes the outgoing history and takes the incoming one, covering both the cache-hit and finished-decode paths with one edit.
- **⚠ `forget_photo_edits()` became `forget_edits_of(path)`**, called only where a history was *actually* discarded: `reload_if_affected`, and `finalize_session` when `take_matching` refuses. Not on every switch any more — that is the whole point.
- **Unbounded map, deliberately.** `BUFFER_CACHE_CAP` is 3 because a decoded buffer is 78–313 MB; a `History<Edits>` is a `Vec` of a few-hundred-byte struct, so 500 photos at 20 steps each is ~4 MB. A cap here would be a constant nobody could justify from a measurement. Histories with nothing to undo are not stashed at all, so the map grows per photo *edited*, not per photo visited.
- **⚠ Redo is per photo too.** A new action clears only the redo entries **it can reach** — the open photo's and global ones. Editing B does not invalidate A's pending redo, because nothing about B makes it wrong; the blanket clear would have reintroduced "your stack is gone" one level down.
- **Tests: 22 in `action_log` (13 pre-existing, unchanged), 7 in `edit_history`.** The reachability tests were **watched failing** — sabotaging `reachable`'s `PhotoEdit` arm to `true` failed seven of them.
- **⚠ The plan missed two sites and the compiler caught them**: `undo_one` and `redo_one` *pattern-match* `ActionKind::PhotoEdit`, not just construct it. Worth remembering when changing a variant's shape — grep for the name, do not enumerate call sites from memory.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) session-only — closing the app loses every stack, since persisting to the sidecar would change the file format, inflate every sidecar by its whole history and raise a migration question; (2) the map is unbounded; (3) a sync to a *closed* photo discards that photo's stack, detected lazily on reopen — the sync itself stays undoable globally; (4) **Ctrl+Z on B can never reach A's edit**, which is the point of the scoping, not a gap; (5) the stash is keyed by path, so moving or renaming a file between visits loses its stack — the same assumption sidecars already make.

**1:1 region rendering done — zoom now re-renders instead of magnifying.**

- **Two render modes.** Fit (`max_dim` = `PREVIEW_MAX_DIM` 1600) and Native (`max_dim` = the editing buffer's long edge, 3123 for the X-T4), switching at **`fit · zoom > 1.0`** — the point where the fit texture is magnified past its own pixels. Quantized to two modes on purpose: a continuously-varying `max_dim` would rebuild the cache on every scroll tick. **Fit mode is byte-for-byte the old behaviour** (`region = None`, same code path).
- **The crop sits between prefix and tail.** The prefix (geometry + NR) runs whole-buffer and is cached; the visible region is cropped out of *its output*, and only the tail runs on it. Two properties made this the right cut: the prefix output **is** the post-geometry image, so screen→region is a plain scale and offset with **no inverse geometry**; and NR having run whole-buffer means **no region boundary can ever produce a seam**, however you pan. That was the deciding argument against cropping first and giving each spatial stage a margin.
- **The cache key does not include the region** — panning re-runs only the tail and never invalidates anything. `CacheKey` stays `(PrefixKey, max_dim)`.
- **Two cache entries** (`PrefixCache::CAP = 2`), one per render mode, so zooming in and out is instant rather than recomputing a 141 ms prefix each way. ~20 MB (fit) + ~78 MB (native).
- **`Region` is optional and additive**: `build_pipeline_split_in_region` / `VignetteStage::new_in_region`, with the existing functions delegating with `None`. **Zero call-site churn** — the CLI, export and all 20 goldens compiled untouched. Rejected alternatives and why: moving vignette into the prefix would run it *before* the tone curve (which would re-map it) and break its "+100 = exactly white" endpoint, besides making every vignette slider invalidate NR; `ImageBuf` carrying `origin`/`full_size` needs no API change but puts a rendering concern in the core type, obliges every future buffer-producing op to maintain it or be silently wrong, and changes `ImageBuf`'s `PartialEq` (which tests compare on); a full `RenderContext` is the cleanest long-term shape but churns every call site for something only zoom uses.
- **⚠ Only `VignetteStage` is position-dependent**, and it is in the tail — so it is the one stage a crop would break. Its `Shape::new` must receive the **frame's** dimensions, not the crop's, or roundness silently reshapes. `a_region_render_matches_the_whole_frame_cropped` and `the_region_sets_the_aspect_not_the_crop` pin both halves.
- **`every_cached_frame_matches_a_cold_render`'s big brother**: `a_region_render_matches_the_whole_render_cropped` asserts `render_region == crop(render_whole)` **bit-identically**, with a vignette, a geometry angle and NR all active. One property validating the region maths, the vignette correction and the cache at once.
- **`app::view_region`** holds the viewport maths as pure, `egui`-free functions (`needs_native`, `visible_rect`, `with_margin`, `contains`) **specifically** so clamping and off-by-one errors are testable rather than only visible as a wrong image. `REGION_MARGIN = 128` px means small pans reuse the existing texture; a re-render is requested only when the visible rect escapes the rendered one.
- **Measured** (X-T4, release, best of 5): native prefix **141.4 ms** one-off; **region tail 1400×900 in 6.9 ms** (plus ~3 ms RGBA pack). Compare the fit-mode cached hit at 13.4 ms.
- **Caveats:** (1) and (2) below are **superseded for RAW** by the full-resolution 1:1 work — see that section. They still describe the **NativeHalf** tier, i.e. zoom between 1.0× and ~1.95× fit, and the whole of a session before the full decode lands. (1) buffer-native is **half** of true full-res, so detail judged there corresponds to ~2× the radius it will have in the export; (2) the half-size decode **skips demosaic entirely** (2×2 binning) while export runs a real algorithm, so that tier differs from the export in *kind*, not only resolution; (3) +78 MB while zoomed; (4) the before/after baseline stays preview-resolution, so toggling it while zoomed shows a softer image; (5) native mode renders slightly more than displayed between 0.51× and 1.0× on-screen scale.

**⚠ Demosaic: we never set `user_qual`, so every decode inherits libraw's slowest.** Measured full-res on the X-T4 (unpack a flat ~850 ms in all cases): bilinear 405 ms → VNG 2,310 → PPG 6,782 → **AHD 10,148** (the default). So ~93% of an 11 s full-res decode is demosaic, chosen by a default nobody made. Two conclusions: **full-res is reachable in ~1.3 s, not 11.4** — which makes a future background full-res upgrade for true 1:1 far more tractable than it looked — and **the planned Phase 5 `wgpu` port would not fix it**, because that time is inside libraw's `dcraw_process`, entirely upstream of the `PipelineStage`s wgpu would touch. GPU demosaic means replacing libraw's, and on X-Trans that is Markesteijn/AMaZE-class work whose errors surface as exactly the fine-detail artifacts one zooms in to inspect. Every export also pays this ~11 s. Tracked as a separate task.

**The fit view now fills the window — and a progressive-sharpen feature was built and then removed.** The visible symptom was a soft fit view on a HiDPI display. The diagnosis took four rounds and three bugs, and the durable lessons are about *diagnosis*, not resolution.

- **⚠ The cause was one line, and not the one I measured.** `let fit = (…).min(1.0)` meant "never magnify past 100%" — but 100% of an arbitrary 1600 px preview, not of the image. On a 2302-point-wide canvas the photo was drawn at **1600 points: letterboxed at 70% of the window**, then upscaled 2× by the display. Removing the clamp fills the window *and* makes the existing zoom ladder climb to the 3200 rung on its own, closing most of the gap with **no new machinery**.
- **⚠ Two things were conflated, and separating them is what made the clamp removal safe.** "Render a region" had meant "the rung is above the fit cap", equivalent to "zoomed in" only *because* `fit` was clamped. Unclamped, the whole image can be visible at a higher rung — and rendering that as a region would starve `fit_image_size`, which is only taken from whole-image frames, leaving it stale after any crop applied outside Geometry mode. Now decided by `view_region::covers_whole_image`.
- **`fit_image_size` is derived from any whole-image frame** by the ratio `fit_cap / frame.max_dim`. Both are *pre-geometry caps*, so the crop factor cancels exactly and neither term comes from a rendered size — it cannot feed back. This replaced an equality test that would silently have rejected the higher-rung frames the clamp fix introduced.
- **⚠⚠ The progressive sharpen was built, shipped, debugged three times, and removed.** Measured: simply raising the cap takes a tail slider **14.6 ms → 45.9 ms (3.1×)** at 2800 px, so it rendered at 1600 while interacting and re-rendered 200 ms after. It never once worked in use:
  1. **It oscillated.** `wants_sharpen` required "the showing resolution is not yet sharp", which goes false the frame the sharp render lands — so the canvas reverted, counted that as interaction, and reset its own timer. Visible as nothing happening.
  2. **It sharpened the *wrong buffer*.** `sharp_cap` clamps to `buffer_long`, which before the full-res upgrade is the **half-size, binned** buffer. Rendering at its native size shows binning artifacts 1:1 that downscaling had been averaging away — no detail gained, ~4× the pixels spent. That was the "slower Exposure slider".
  3. **The clamp fix made its gate unreachable.** It tested "at the base rung", true only while `fit` was clamped.
  With the gate finally right the residual was 1.44× → 1:1 sampling for ~2× the render, and **the user could not see it on or off**. Removed: `sharpen.rs`, the interaction tracking, the `FINALS_CAP` bump, the debug print.
- **⚠ The lesson: one print settled what four rounds of reasoning had not.** The opening measurement was real but measured a *consequence*. Nobody checked what the fit view was actually doing on that display until an env-gated `eprintln` went in, and its first line (`native=true` — the user was never at the fit rung) reframed the problem. Reach for instrumentation earlier when the code reads correctly but the behaviour disagrees.
- The design doc survives as `2026-08-13-progressive-fit-resolution-design.md`, marked **built, then removed**, carrying the measurements and the post-mortem.

**Rotate and flip done — the Crop panel is now the Geometry panel.** Quarter turns and mirroring, the eight rigid transforms of a rectangle. Not in any milestone: the capability inventory lists "rotate" but the P0–P4 table never scheduled it.

- **Two fields, eight states, no redundancy.** `Orientation { rotate: Quarter, mirror: bool }` — mirror about the vertical axis, *then* turn. ⚠ **`mirror` is one flag, not two**: a vertical flip *is* a horizontal flip plus a half turn, so separate `flip_h`/`flip_v` fields would make four states representable two ways. **Flip-vertical is a derived action**, so the UI has four buttons over two fields.
- **⚠ Composition is non-abelian, and this is where it silently breaks.** Rotating composes trivially, but flipping *after* a rotation does not: `flip_h` is `rotate = -rotate; mirror = !mirror`, because `M ∘ rot_r = rot_{-r} ∘ M`. Get it wrong and every button is right in isolation and wrong in sequence.
- **⚠⚠ The obvious property test does not catch that — verified, not assumed.** "The generators reach exactly 8 states" *passes* with the wrong abelian rule, because it still reaches all eight, just as a direct product rather than a dihedral group. The count cannot distinguish the two groups. The real guard is **`each_button_matches_its_geometric_action`**, which checks each button against an **independent corner model** — where the unit square's corners land — from all eight starting states. Sabotaged `flip_h` and watched: the count test passed, the geometric test failed. *A property test is only as good as the property.*
- **⚠ The crop follows the rotation.** `CropRect::rotated_cw` / `flipped_h`, applied alongside every orientation button — without them, rotating a cropped photo leaves the crop on entirely different pixels. In the unit square a clockwise turn is `(x, y) → (1 − y, x)`. ⚠ Round trips are exact only to `f32`: `1.0 - (1.0 - 0.1)` is 0.100000024, so the four-turn test uses a 1e-6 tolerance — a millionth of the frame, sub-pixel on any image.
- **Lossless.** An index permutation, no resampling; `orient()` inverse-maps each output pixel. The mirror is undone in *source* space after un-rotating, so `w` is the source width whatever the turn.
- **⚠ The dimension swap needed no work elsewhere**, which is worth recording because it looks like it should have: `fit_cap` uses the *buffer's* long edge (a swap does not change `max(w,h)`), `k` compares two caps, the vignette's `Region` comes from the prefix output's real dimensions, and `fit_image_size` is already derived from the returned frame.
- **Goldens 29 → 31**, and ⚠ **verified numerically against `neutral.png`, not by squinting**: `orient_cw90` *is* a quarter turn clockwise, and `orient_mirror` (180° + mirror) nets to a **vertical** flip and is *not* a horizontal one — the non-abelian composition confirmed end to end on real pixels. The 29 existing stayed byte-identical.
- **The rename:** `crop_mode` → `geometry_mode` and both labels. ⚠ `preview_edits` still resets the crop so the whole frame shows under the overlay, and must **not** reset the orientation — the preview would otherwise be oriented differently from the image being edited.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) rotating requires entering Geometry mode, as Straighten already did; (2) it lives inside `GeometryStage` rather than earning its own prefix checkpoint — 313 MB at full resolution to avoid re-running a cheap, rarely-changed transform; (3) a *flip* mirrors the crop with the frame, so a deliberately asymmetric crop ends up on the other side; (4) older sidecars load with the identity orientation via `#[serde(default)]`.

**Library format filter done — a RAW+JPEG folder can be browsed one format at a time.** A chip per extension actually present, in the top toolbar.

- **A derived index list, not a filtered `entries`.** `Library`'s `entries`, `thumbs`, `Selection` and `ThumbLoader` are all **index-parallel**, so filtering the list itself would mean rebuilding `thumbs`, remapping every stored index and restarting the loader — on every toggle. Instead `entries` stays whole and `visible: Vec<usize>` is derived; toggling is a few hundred `usize` and no thumbnail reload. The cost is that the grid, filmstrip and both arrow-nav sites iterate `visible` rather than `0..len`.
- **⚠ The feature's real content is the selection, not the display.** Select twenty photos, hide their format, and the selection still holds them — a sync or paste then writes to photos that are not on screen. Silent, and discovered as data loss rather than as a bug. `Library::refilter` therefore always does **both** halves: recompute `visible` *and* `Selection::retain_visible`.
- **⚠ `retain_visible` follows `clamp`'s rule rather than inventing a second one.** If the active photo is pruned, the whole selection goes. Two rules for "the active index went away" is a smell, and silently *moving* the active photo changes which photo a sync copies **from**, with nothing on screen to announce it. The design doc originally said "move to a survivor"; reading `clamp` corrected it.
- **⚠ Ctrl+A is the deliberate exception**, and `select_all(n)` became `select_indices(&[usize])` because "all" now means "all visible" and the visible list is *sparse*. It adopts a new active photo when the current one is outside the set — defensible because Ctrl+A is an explicit "select these" from the user, where a filter change is a side effect. Both behaviours are pinned by tests that name the distinction.
- **⚠ It stores what is *hidden*, not what is shown.** With a shown-set, a format appearing after a folder reload would be absent from the set and therefore silently invisible. Hidden-by-exception makes "everything visible" the default and needs no maintenance.
- **⚠ The grid's row break must count the cell's *position*, not the entry index** — found in use, one toggle later. `egui::Grid` broke rows on `(i + 1) % cols`, which is right only while `i` is dense; with a filter on it fires after the wrong cells, giving gaps and rows that overflow the window. This is the **third** sparse-vs-dense index bug in this lineage (after the crop feedback loop and `render_max_dim`), so it has a test: `the_grid_breaks_rows_by_position_not_by_entry_index` lays out a sparse list both ways and asserts the index-counted one is *wrong*. ⚠ Like `cell_widths` beside it, that test reimplements the loop rather than driving the real one — it pins the rule, not the call site.
- **⚠ …and a second, pre-existing layout bug the filter merely made visible: the column count ignored egui's inter-column gaps.** `n` cells need `n*CELL + (n-1)*spacing`, not `n*CELL`. ⚠ **The failure rate rises with window width** — the gap total grows with the column count while the leftover width stays uniform in `[0, CELL)` — which is why it showed on a large monitor and not a small one, and why it had gone unnoticed. `grid_columns(avail, cell, spacing)` is pure and swept over widths by `grid_columns_leaves_room_for_the_gaps`, which asserts both that the chosen count fits *and* that one more would not.
- **⚠ `format_filter::step` exists because Develop keeps the open photo when its format is hidden** — so arrow navigation must cope with a `cur` that is *not* in the visible list, taking the nearest visible index in the direction of travel. Clamped at both ends rather than wrapping.
- **Three UI calls:** chips appear only at **≥2 formats** (a lone toggle can only blank the grid); the **filmstrip is filtered too**, since it is the same list and the same intent; the filter **resets on folder load** rather than persisting — a `Settings` field is an obvious follow-up if "always RAW only" turns out to be wanted.
- Extensions compare lowercased, so `.JPG` and `.jpg` are one chip; an extensionless file is always visible, since no control exists for it.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) the filter is per-session; (2) hidden photos stay in `BufferCache` if visited — it is a view, not an eviction; (3) thumbnails for hidden entries still load, which is what keeps toggling instant.

**Three bugs from the first hour of real use (2026-08-12), and the two interesting ones are mine.** Recorded together because the lessons are about *inference vs. fact*, not about the individual symptoms.

- **⚠ "Reset all" did not stick across a photo switch.** `persist_current_session` wrote the sidecar only when `edits != default`, so a reset wrote nothing and **left the old file on disk** to be loaded straight back. `sidecar::remove` already existed and was simply never called. Fixed by `sidecar::persist`, which deletes on neutral. This had been documented as a "minor edge" caveat for weeks — it is a reminder that a caveat nobody has hit is just a bug nobody has hit *yet*.
- **⚠⚠ One photo opened shrinking to a few pixels, unrecoverably. The cause was a feedback loop I built, and the crop is why only one photo showed it.** The render ladder's base rung was `fit_long`, derived from `fit_image_size` — but that is the **post-geometry** size, while `downscale_to`'s cap is **pre-geometry**. Geometry crops *after* the downscale, so with a 0.588 crop: render at cap 1600 → frame 941 → that becomes the next cap → 553 → 325 → 191 → … one step per frame. **Uncropped photos have a crop factor of 1 and sit at a fixed point**, so the bug was invisible on 400 files and total on the one cropped one. `view_region::fit_cap(buffer_long, preview_max)` is now the base and depends only on the buffer.
  - **⚠ The same confusion had a second, older instance:** `k` compared a pre-geometry cap against a post-geometry size, so native-zoom placement was off by `1/crop_factor` on any cropped photo — a pre-existing bug this fix also closed. Both sides are caps now, so the crop cancels.
  - **⚠ The user's phrasing was the diagnosis.** "Zooms out *rapidly*" means a loop, not a bad value; "one frame at the right size, then snaps small again" identified the forced post-upgrade render at a fixed cap feeding the loop's next iteration. Symptom sequences are evidence — read them literally.
- **⚠ `render_max_dim` must be *derived* from `requested_region`, never stored beside it.** It began as a method and I turned it into a field during the trigger rework; two facts that can disagree then did, when `pump_full_res` cleared the region while the canvas had left a native rung. A panel edit then issued a whole-image render at a native rung, which poisoned the cached fit size. Deriving one from the other makes the disagreement **unrepresentable** — better than testing for it.
- **⚠ `RenderedFrame` now carries its `max_dim`, because "whole image ⟹ fit render" is an inference that a crop can falsify.** A native rung times a small crop factor lands *below* the preview cap, so a size-based guard fails open exactly in the case that caused the bug. Twice bitten by this inference; it is a field now. Every whole-image request uses `Session::fit_cap()` so the comparison is exact — normalising that also exposed an inconsistency for images smaller than the preview cap.
- **⚠ A found-in-passing bug: the full-resolution upgrade was not re-rendering at fit zoom at all.** The rung was unchanged and the region already covered, so the canvas requested nothing and the old buffer's texture stayed on screen — the upgrade was invisible unless you happened to be zoomed in. `pump_full_res` now issues an explicit request.

**`downscale_to` is 3.2× faster — the last sequential hot loop in the render path is gone.** Parallel across **output rows**: each output pixel averages its own source rectangle and reads nothing else, so the result is bit-identical.

- **Measured** (X-T4, release, best of 9): `downscale_to(1600)` from the half buffer **9.1 → 2.8 ms**; from a full-resolution buffer **27.3 → 8.6 ms**; `downscale_to(3123)` from full is 10.8 ms.
- **⚠ Consequence for the pending trigger rework:** sourcing the *fit* tier from the full-resolution buffer now costs **8.6 ms, less than today's 9.1 ms** from the half one. The +18 ms regression that rework was going to pay simply does not exist any more — do it before, not after.
- **Rows, not the fixed blocks `to_rgba8` uses**, and that is a deliberate difference: a 1600-wide output row already averages tens of thousands of source pixels, so there is none of the per-item dispatch overhead that made a per-pixel split of the 8-bit pack *slower than not threading at all*. The granularity was checked rather than assumed, which is the lesson that work left behind.
- **⚠ It found a pre-existing bug on the way in.** `dw`/`dh` are clamped up to 1, so a 0×5 source produced a **1×2 image of `NaN`** — the averaging loop found no source pixels and divided by zero. The empty-dimension guard fixes that *and* is what makes the row chunk width provably non-zero, which `par_chunks_exact_mut` requires. `downscale_handles_the_degenerate_sizes` pins both.
- **Bit-identity is proven against a sequential reference written inline in the tests**, over five source/output ratios chosen to be non-integral in both axes with more rows than any plausible thread count. Watched to fail by perturbing the row index.

**True full resolution done — every zoom now shows the real pixels.** What Rustroom called "100%" was a **half-size** decode that *skips demosaic entirely* (2×2 binning) while export runs a real algorithm. A full decode now starts in the background ~0.8 s after a photo settles, and every rung of the render ladder uses it. ⚠ This section describes the **reworked** version; it shipped zoom-triggered with two engines first, and the rework is the most instructive part.

- **Measured** (X-T4 `.RAF`, release, `Demosaic::Fast`):

  | | value |
  |---|---|
  | half decode (unchanged) | 985 ms → 3123×2085, 78 MB |
  | **full decode** | **3026 ms** → 6246×4170, **313 MB** |
  | `downscale_to(1600)` from half / from full | 9.1 ms / **27.3 ms** — *now 2.8 / 8.6, see below* |
  | geometry (angle) whole-buffer at full res | 203 ms |
  | noise reduction 50/50 at full res | 504 ms |
  | dehaze 60 at full res | 562 ms |
  | **region tail 1400×900 at full res** | **13.4 ms** |

- **⚠ The trigger was zoom-based first, and using it proved that wrong.** Shipped that way, measured after, reworked the same day. Compared against a properly demosaiced render: **Fit differs in 5.5% of samples (mean 0.91/255), NativeHalf in 22.6% (mean 1.69, max 211)**. So the gap is *not* at fit — it is at 1.0–1.95× zoom, an ordinary editing zoom that never crosses a 1.95× threshold. The decode is now **eager, once the session settles**. For reference: Lightroom's Develop module always works from a full decode; its Library previews are a separate browsing cache, and Smart Previews (2560 px lossy DNG) are its one opt-in downgrade.
- **⚠ A ladder, not an enum — the first version was three fixed tiers and it did not generalise.** `tier_max_dim(fit_long, buffer_long, fit, zoom)` walks rungs that **double** from fit resolution up to the buffer's own. A 3123 buffer yields two rungs (exactly the pre-feature behaviour), a 6246 one yields three, any sensor, with nothing to extend when the buffer is upgraded underneath it. Rungs double so on-screen scale cannot fall below ~0.5; they are quantized so `max_dim` does not vary continuously with zoom, which would rebuild the prefix cache on every scroll tick.
- **⚠ Why the ladder exists at all, and it is not obvious.** The tempting design — "native mode just uses the full-res buffer" — is quietly disastrous. Native mode's `max_dim` was one value keeping scale in (0.51, 1.0]; doubling the buffer **halves** that to ~0.26, and the region is derived from the viewport **in rendered pixels**, so a 1400×900 viewport would need a **5385×3462** region — 18.6 Mpx instead of 1.26, turning the 13.4 ms tail into **~180 ms**. The feature would have made slider response *worse* across most of its own zoom range.
- **⚠ State the invariant precisely or a test written from it fails.** "Scale stays in (0.5, 1.0]" is false twice over: at the fit rung there is no lower bound (zoomed out, fit is arbitrarily small), and at the *top* rung scale exceeds 1.0 because the file holds no more data. Testable form: **for a rung above fit and below the buffer, scale ∈ (0.5, 1.0]**. `a_rung_below_the_buffer_never_magnifies` sweeps both buffer sizes.
- **One engine, whose buffer is replaced.** The two-engine version (half buffer for fit, full for 1:1) was right only while fit kept using the half buffer; once every rung renders from the full one, the half engine is dead weight. Dropping the old engine disconnects its channel so its worker returns on its own. ⚠ **Its prefix cache goes too** — a one-off cost at the moment of upgrade. The rework removed **87 lines net**.
- **⚠ The dwell is not redundant with the 180 ms open debounce.** There is already an implicit ~1.2 s before a `Session` exists (debounce plus a ~1 s half decode) — but a **`BufferCache` hit creates a `Session` instantly**, so arrowing *back* through recently viewed photos would fire a 3 s, 313 MB decode each. `full_res::SETTLE` is 800 ms of session age.
- **⚠ The trigger must schedule its own repaint.** egui does not redraw an idle window, so without `request_repaint_after` the dwell could expire with nothing to notice it and the decode would start only when the user next moved the mouse.
- **⚠ Handover clears `rendered_region` and `requested_region`.** Region rects are in *rendered* pixels, and those coordinates change with the buffer; reusing one would describe the wrong part of the image.
- **Reuses `DecodeWorker`** (second instance, `max_concurrent: 1`, `DecodeOptions::linear_scene()` injected) — abandon-don't-abort, sequence numbers and all four of its tests, for one constructor call. The baseline it renders is discarded so before/after does not change under the user. A failed decode leaves the ladder topping out at the half-size buffer, i.e. the pre-feature behaviour.
- **Cache: `MID_BUDGET_BYTES` 256 → 512 MiB, plus a new `MIDS_CAP = 6`.** 512 clears one full-res checkpoint (~313 MB) but not two — deliberate, since a second buys ~200 ms on a rare interaction for double the memory. ⚠ **512 rather than 320 because 320 covers only 28.0 Mpx**, and Fuji's current 40.2 Mpx bodies (X-T5, X-H2) would get *zero* native checkpoints; 512 covers 44.7 Mpx. The sizing is a **compile-time assertion**, not a comment. ⚠ **The count cap fixes a pre-existing leak**: dragging Straighten mints a new post-geometry key every frame, so the mids tier filled with stale checkpoints bounded only by bytes — a bigger budget would simply have retained more garbage. A chain needs at most 3 mids, so 6 keeps it plus one previous state.
- **Memory: ~1.32 GB worst case** for one photo zoomed in with all three prefix stages active (78 half buffer + 156 other cache entries + 313 full buffer + 147 fit cache + 626 native cache).
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) **~3 s of background CPU and 313 MB per photo you stop on**, whether or not you zoom — the price of being correct by default; (2) ~1.32 GB worst case, now reached on any photo you dwell on rather than only zoomed ones; (3) **between opening and the upgrade landing (~4 s) every rung is still the binned half-size decode** — better than "until you zoom past 1.95×", but not instant; (4) the prefix cache is lost at the moment of upgrade, so the first render after it is cold; (5) prefix sliders cost ~0.2–0.6 s per frame at the top rung — one checkpoint softens it, the rest is the price of a whole-buffer prefix, and tail sliders stay at 13.4 ms; (6) ~~the view uses `Demosaic::Fast`, not the export preset's setting~~ — **resolved 2026-08-13, but not as stated**: the tier is now `Settings.view_demosaic`, an explicit choice defaulting to `Fast`. Mirroring the export preset was considered and **rejected**; see the view-quality section above for the three reasons; (7) switching photos and back re-decodes, since the full-res buffer is not cached across sessions; (8) the before/after baseline stays preview-resolution.

**The 8-bit pack is 10× faster — and the task's own name was wrong about why.** `render_engine::pack` was the largest slice of a cached preview frame; it is now the smallest.

- **Measured** (X-T4 `.RAF`, release, best of 9, 8 threads). **Two independent multipliers**, kept as separate commits so the credit goes where it is due:

  | | fit 1600×1068 | native 3123×2085 |
  |---|---|---|
  | `Vec::push` loop (the original) | 4.59 ms | 17.81 ms |
  | preallocate + sequential fill | **1.16 ms** | 4.42 ms |
  | + parallel in 8192-px blocks | **0.46 ms** | 2.21 ms |

- **⚠ Most of the win was not parallelism.** The task was named "parallelise the RGBA8 pack", but the measurement that started it showed `export::to_rgb8` doing ¾ as many output bytes on the same buffer in ⅕ the time — **3.7× cheaper per byte, with no thread involved**. `Vec::push` carries a capacity check per byte and does not vectorise; `.collect()` over an exact-size iterator allocates once. Dropping the push was a 4.0× win on its own, threading a further 2.5×.
- **⚠ The obvious parallel spelling is *slower than not threading at all*.** `par_chunks_exact_mut(4)` — one pixel per rayon task — measured **1.64 ms against 1.13 ms sequential**, because per-item dispatch stops the inner work vectorising. Coarse blocks with a plain sequential loop inside get both: **0.46 ms**. `PACK_BLOCK_PX = 8192`, and the plateau is flat from ~1024 up (52.8 / 58.9 / 61.3 GB/s at 1024 / 8192 / 65536), so it sits mid-plateau rather than on a tuned peak. **This also disproves the tempting explanation** that the operation is memory-bandwidth-bound and untreadable — 61 GB/s against 24 says otherwise. Both wrong shapes are recorded in the `PACK_BLOCK_PX` doc comment so nobody re-derives them.
- **One quantizer, on `ImageBuf`.** `to_rgb8` / `to_rgba8` share a private `quantize`. `export::to_rgb8` keeps its name and signature and delegates, so no call site churned and its existing `to_rgb8_quantizes_and_clamps` became the guard that delegation preserved behaviour. **Every export got faster for free** — it was the same sequential code. **`app` still has no `rayon` dependency**, preserving the separation recorded for `ThumbLoader`.
- **⚠ The golden harness keeps its own private copy, deliberately.** A golden that quantizes through production code cannot witness a change to production code. That third copy is confined to a test, where drift is a visible failure rather than wrong pixels — and it is precisely what let the 29 checked-in PNGs act as an independent check on this change.
- **⚠ No zero-size guard here, unlike `map_pixels_xy` — and that is correct.** The block widths are non-zero constants, so a zero-width image just yields an empty `Vec`. `map_pixels_xy` chunks by *row*, which is 0 for a zero-width image, and `par_chunks_exact_mut(0)` panics. Chunking by a fixed block rather than a row is what buys the difference. Pinned by `the_conversions_handle_an_empty_image`.
- **⚠ Bit-identity could not be proven by the existing tests.** `every_cached_frame_matches_a_cold_render` compares two frames that would *both* go through the new pack. So `core-image`'s tests carry **sequential reference loops written inline**, deliberately not shared with production, on a fixture with negative, >1, `NaN` and exact-rounding-boundary samples. Watched to fail (both reference tests) with `dst[3] = 254`.
- **⚠ `NaN` reaches 0 via the `as u8` cast, not the clamp.** `f32::clamp` returns `NaN` for `NaN`; Rust's float→int casts saturate. Noted in `quantize`'s doc because "clamp handles NaN" is the obvious wrong reading.
- **Accepted caveats:** (1) a third copy of the quantization lives in the golden harness, on purpose; (2) `vec![0u8; n]` writes every byte twice — 0.09 ms at fit, measured and accepted rather than fixed with unsafe; (3) the pack now competes for the global rayon pool, but it runs on the render worker where the pipeline stages already do; (4) gains shrink on small images where rayon's overhead is a larger fraction, which only affects tests.

**Per-stage prefix checkpoints done — a Dehaze drag is ~1.9× faster.** The prefix is no longer cached as one lump; every stage boundary is its own checkpoint, so a change re-runs only what is downstream of it.

- **Measured first, and the measurement corrected the plan twice** (X-T4 `.RAF`, release, best of 5). Fit = 1600 px / 21 MB per buffer; native = 3123 px / 78 MB.

  | step | fit | native |
  |---|---|---|
  | `downscale_to` | 9.0 ms | **2.2 ms** (already small enough — it is a clone) |
  | geometry, angle 3° | **13.6 ms** | **52.2 ms** |
  | geometry, crop only | 1.0 ms | 3.9 ms |
  | noise reduction 50/50 | 31.4 ms | 117.4 ms |
  | dehaze 60 | 32.3 ms | 115.6 ms |
  | deep clone of one checkpoint | 0.5 ms | 2.1 ms |

  And the resume points, with all three prefix stages active:

  | what you drag | resumes at | cost |
  |---|---|---|
  | geometry / Straighten | the downscale | 101.2 ms |
  | noise reduction | after geometry | 87.5 ms |
  | **Dehaze** | **after NR** | **49.7 ms** (was 95.4) |
  | any tail slider | the final | 14.4 ms |

  Reproduce with `cargo run --release -p app --example bench_preview <raw>`.
- **⚠ Correcting this file, again — the post-geometry checkpoint is worth far more than the old RESUME block claimed.** That note said a second checkpoint's only win was the ~9 ms downscale, reasoning about *changing* Straighten (where it is right: geometry is first, so changing it invalidates everything by definition). But the checkpoint's real job is the other direction — with an angle set, every NR or Dehaze drag was re-running a **13.6 ms / 52.2 ms** rotation. Note the asymmetry inside geometry: a crop alone is ~1 ms, so essentially all of that is the rotation.
- **⚠ The post-downscale checkpoint is free at native, and would have been a terrible deal otherwise.** `downscale_to` returns a *clone* when the image is already small enough, so caching checkpoint 0 natively would cost 78 MB to save 2.2 ms. The engine already holds the buffer as `Arc<ImageBuf>`, so the chain seeds from that Arc and stores nothing.
